Beck: Clay, what do you think the thing is that people who are nostalgic for the ’90s or the 2000s are wanting to bring forward and revise, as you say? Routledge: If I could sum it up using two words. Routledge: I would say control and community. Routledge: And the reason I’m picking those two words is because we’ve done, in addition to the surveys, we’ve done a number of qualitative projects that, you know, look at the types of nostalgic-related posts that Gen Z adults make on different social media and websites that they frequent. And what you often find is this sense that they love technology, but they wanna be in control of it, not feel controlled by it. Routledge: So it’s that more sense of intentional use, and this is why I think you see these interesting trends like Gen Z buying old iPods and, you know, they wanna have their new iPhone. Everybody wants to have the Uber app and all the different things that make our lives a little more convenient, but sometimes there’s a sense of like, Maybe there’s a more low-tech option that allows me just to listen to music. Routledge: And the community. I think Gen Z’s figuring out that there’s something missing in computer-mediated social relationships. And on the one hand, the social media’s really useful ’cause it helps you organize events, meet people you would’ve never met. But at the same time, they look at this time period right before smartphones really became dominant, and they said, Oh, there’s a sense of freedom. There’s a sense of organic social life, and— Beck: You’re not looking at your phone the whole time. Routledge: —mm hmm. And they want to build that in. A lot of times, nostalgia’s framed in a very zero-sum sort of framework, where it’s like: You’re either for the past or you’re for the future. And I think what Gen Z’s showing us is they want both. And there’s something to say intergenerationally about this, which is maybe young people are learning about ’90s fashions on TikTok, but then what we often find is that sparks a conversation with older generations, whether it’s a parent or a coworker you know, or somebody in their run club, that they’re like, Oh, was life really like this? Did you really do this? And it creates a dialogue between generations, which I think, in this era of where everyone has their own screens and their own tailored content, is something that people feel like they’re really missing. Beck: Yeah. I remember my dad always used to talk about that—that sort of, like, shared experience of music, ’cause you had to, like, put on a record, and so everybody’s listening to the Rolling Stones whether they like it or not. And now, obviously, we just have our sort of, like, isolated pods, and you don’t know that I’m listening to that same Taylor Swift song like 10 times in a row, and you’ll never know. Beck: Okay. Yeah, I did—yeah, now everybody knows. Beck: We’re gonna take a quick break. When we come back: Why is this analog nostalgia bubbling up now? Do you think it has anything to do with AI coming on the scene? Beck: Clay, why do you think nostalgia for analog technology is so popular right now? Does the buzz about AI have anything to do with it? Routledge: I think AI is part of it, because if machines can do all this cognitive work, and maybe even creative work, that we thought was the distinct domain of humanity, now you’re seeing more of this holistic thinking of like, what is the essence of being a person? Routledge: And this nostalgia, I think, is really helping people get that sense of embodiment, of like, this isn’t just cognition. There’s something about emotion, spirituality, living in the world, having these tactile sensory experiences, in-person, real contact with other real human beings. Beck: Just with like, all the parts of life that are more than just being a brain in a jar, basically? Routledge: Yeah, that’s a great way of putting it. And if you look at a lot of the consumer nostalgic trends, many of them driven by Gen Z, they’re very much that, right? They’re the rise of independent bookstores, the growth of music festivals, book clubs, game nights, old tech, technology that’s not online that you have to—I was talking to somebody the other day about retro video games, and they were saying, “Well, one thing that’s really cool is you have to sit next to each other to play, like two players.” Routledge: You’re not playing online. And so I think there’s this idea that even if it’s technology, even if it’s like video games or music, you mentioned vinyl records, which, you know, one of the big drivers of vinyl-record sales is Gen Z. They’re these low-tech, retro-tech trends that really seem to be about being out in the world, handling things, engaging with people. That’s a big driver of this. Beck: Natalie, let’s play a little game where I name a thing that people might be nostalgic for, and then try to psychoanalyze what it means about what they actually want from their relationship to technology. Beck: Okay, my first one: I see a lot of memes about people missing the days when people had computer rooms in their home. Like, picture a big wooden entertainment center with a big monitor. The shelves are all covered in CD-ROMs, and then the meme says something like, We used to respect the computer. What does this mean? Brennan: This is really giving that control element that Clay was talking about— Brennan: —in that we still want to honor our computer. We love computer time, right? But I think we miss that separation in which your technology wasn’t on you at all times, right? My phone is kind of just a mini computer at this point. Brennan: So I think we like having some separation of, like, now it’s time for me to go in the computer room, and when I’m done, it’s done. Does that resonate with you? Beck: Yeah. It’s a deliberate choice to go in the computer room and sit your butt in front of it and use it and then leave. Beck: I think you’re onto something. Okay, I am personally very nostalgic for Google Reader— Beck: —which, for those who may not know, it was an aggregation service for websites’ RSS feeds, which doesn't sound fun, but it was. Beck: Um, basically, in the late 2000s, I would use this to, like, follow all my favorite blogs and cooking websites and news sources, and it was all in one place. What do you think I’m actually longing for here? Brennan:  Okay, well, I think for me what comes up here is feeling like the internet isn’t anymore curated towards what I wanna see. So for example, like I’ll open my Instagram app and I no longer see posts by my friends and family who I follow, but instead I see a lot of pushed posts. The algorithm just sends me what it thinks I wanna see. And so maybe you’re yearning for that time where the internet was curated to your niche hobbies and what you are actually interested in. Beck: As opposed to just a bunch of slop. Brennan: Okay, I wanna ask you one. I am also one of the analog Gen Zers who love to shoot film and collect old cameras. Thoughts? Beck: Um, I don’t know about you, but sometimes I pick up my phone to take a picture of probably my cats, and before I know it, I’m, like, sucked into a million other things on my phone. Beck: So I think, maybe, there’s also something about, like, when you wanna take a picture, you just wanna take a picture, and taking that function out of the everything device and letting it just be its own thing, maybe. Beck: Clay, I was fascinated to learn that back in the 17th to 19th century, nostalgia was considered a disease, and doctors tried to cure it with leeches. I read that there was this one American doctor who kind of thought the way to cure it was just bullying people, like shaming them with public ridicule. And, obviously, we no longer think nostalgia is literally a disease, but I think a thread of that negative attitude remains. I feel like I sometimes hear other people talk about nostalgia as though you’re trying to hide from reality, and maybe, you know, you should be a little embarrassed. Do you see that reaction from people when it comes to this current cultural yearning for a pre-digital era that, okay, it’s not a disease, but maybe it’s still not good for you? Routledge: Yeah, definitely. People seem to have this view of nostalgia as anti-progress or holding people back from progress, and I spend a fair amount of time trying to disabuse them of that view and show them decades of empirical research. It really isn’t holding people back. We all have to adjust to change, and nostalgia helps people navigate that in the best way that they can. Even people who don’t think nostalgia’s a barrier to progress, oftentimes you see it characterized as a waste of time. Routledge: Like, it’s a trivial thing, and it’s a distraction at best. Actually, nostalgia increases goal motivation. It increases a sense of meaning in life. It increases optimism. It makes people more creative. It increases empathy. Most nostalgic memories are social memories, so nostalgia actually pushes us towards each other. Beck: When you’re saying that some people are seeing nostalgia as an impediment to progress, do you think some of that has to do with the sort of Make America Great Again brand of nostalgia where, like you’re saying, people are looking to the past to potentially bring things into the future, but sometimes what they want to bring into the future is, for instance, stricter and more stereotypical gender roles? Routledge: So declinism is the view that things are getting worse and the past was better, whereas nostalgia really is just more of this warm feeling for the past, and I think the two are often conflated. We also find, overwhelmingly, that most of the time, nostalgia in general makes us see the humanity in other people. Or another way of saying it is: Nostalgia might be helping people wrestle with declinism— Routledge: —and hopefully in a more productive and constructive way. A lot of Gen Z might feel like, oh, things are going in the wrong direction and their future’s gonna be worse than the future of previous generations. And there's certainly, you know, a lot of data that support that. Nostalgia’s helping them feel agentic and say, Well, you know what? We can change that. We can do something else. Beck: It just feels surprising when you see an era that was not a great time for a lot of people recast as something to be nostalgic for. Routledge: There are people that have nostalgia for COVID, which, again, not that long ago. Routledge: Not that fun. But you know what? What’s interesting is, that wasn’t that surprising to me. When I first started doing this research, I was a professor at the University of Southampton in England. And Southampton’s a city that was heavily bombed during World War II, as a port city, and a good chunk of the city was destroyed. Not a good time. When we were doing this research, this was a little over 20 years ago, we were collecting nostalgic narratives from older British adults. Many of them were young children during that time, and they had this nostalgia. Routledge: But what it was is they were saying, Well, this was a time that was defining, character defining. It wasn’t a pleasant time. It shaped our character, shaped our view of the world, shaped our ability to appreciate what’s important in life. Beck: So how does this sort of personal, individual nostalgia, right, then grow and end up becoming a cultural moment or an internet trend? Routledge: ’Cause a lot of times what people are really looking for with nostalgia is an opportunity to share memories. Routledge: Oftentimes, nostalgia is portrayed as this very isolating, personal experience. After all, this is in your head. You’re experiencing this emotion. But people love opportunities where they can talk about nostalgic experiences, particularly ones where they might have their own idiosyncratic memory of it, but it was shared with other people. Routledge: I think this is why people have a nostalgia for television where you had to wait and everyone had to watch the show at the same time, and then the next day we could all talk about it. Routledge: You know, people are looking for ways to make contact over shared experiences where their personal nostalgia is woven into a broader cultural nostalgia that connects us. Beck: And we’ve talked about control a good amount, and, you know, obviously we can’t actually travel back in time to an era when nobody had smartphones. Sam Altman is gonna keep making new iterations of ChatGPT, and I have no control to stop him. But do the things that we’re nostalgic for hold any lessons for how we can exert the control that we do have over the role tech plays in our lives? Routledge: When the broader world outside of yourself, when you scale out, when it feels like you don’t have any control over that, that can be anxiety provoking; if you just focus your attention on your corner of the world, we actually do have a lot more control. Agency often happens at a local and at a personal level. You can help your neighbors. You can help your family. You can help your friends. You can be involved with those things. And even media engagement’s a way to remind ourselves of that. It’s like, Yeah, I don’t have control over the next internet iteration of a particular technology, but I can go to that retro store down the street and buy some Blu-ray discs and have a movie night at my house and have some people over. Routledge: So I think people are really seeking that kind of experience. Beck:  Are you seeing people actually acting on this pre-digital nostalgia? Routledge: There is an increased sales in physical media. The live-music market is growing. Mass attendance is up, like young people going to church. Routledge: The number of independent bookstores and the number of chain bookstores, like Barnes & Nobles, is growing. There does seem to be something changing in our culture that’s a push back to the in-person and the analog. Beck:  Clay, I’ve noticed at many times in this conversation that you’re very optimistic about Gen Z, which is not how I often hear people talking about that generation, especially when it comes to tech. So I’m curious to hear a little bit more about how you’re thinking about it and whether Gen Z is kind of leading this trend for other generations. Routledge: My thinking on Gen Z has really evolved, so I’m way more bullish on Gen Z— Routledge: —than I used to be. So I’m a psychologist, and so when all of this research was coming out about these growing mental-health problems that young people were facing and how Gen Z reported higher rates of depression, anxiety, loneliness, pessimism for the future, like everyone else, I was like, This isn’t good. Young people are supposed to be the ones that are optimistic. They’re supposed to be the ones that look to the future with possibility, and that didn’t seem to be the case. But then I started really studying this historical nostalgia and started seeing, like, yeah, Gen Z has their challenges, and maybe mental health is one of them, but they’re not just sitting there doing nothing about it. They’re using their creativity and their problem-solving capacities that all humans have to do something about it, and I feel like this historical nostalgia that they’re engaging in is just one example of that. It’s not people my age that remember that time. It’s Gen Z that are saying, Hey, maybe we’re spending too much time online. And so, for instance, there’s surveys showing that Gen Z are the generation most likely to report disconnecting from their digital devices. Routledge: So now I have a different view. It’s a, you know, it’s not a certain view, of course. It’s an optimistic view, though, that Gen Z might not only be doing better than we think they are in navigating their challenges and having the agency to do so, but they actually might lead us out of this mess. They might be the ones that remind the rest of us that there is an in-person, real-world life. So, you know, I often say that it’s common for people to feel sorry for Gen Z and worry that as digital natives, they were born too late to really appreciate the analog world. Routledge: But in reality, it might be that they’re the generation that leads us back to it. Beck: It sounds like you’re not so sure, Natalie. Brennan: I said I wanted to throw my phone in the East River, and suddenly now I am tasked with healing everyone’s relationship to technology. Beck: Not just you. Just you and your entire generation are responsible for the rest of us? Uh … Brennan: I don’t know. I get so frustrated about this because I feel like I hear it all the time. It’s the same thing with climate change. Like, We’ll be the ones that need to fix all of it. I’m just like, This list is getting a little bit long. We’ve got a lot to fix now. Beck:  Honestly, I think what talking to Clay made me realize is that so many of us are just looking for inspiration on how to move forward with technology in a way that doesn’t feel bad. Because we have to live in 2026. Brennan: Right. I think as a joint mission, then I can get behind that. Beck: When, like, Gen Z doesn’t have to be the leader, nobody’s the leader, nobody’s the follower, we’re just figuring it out together. Brennan: Yeah, or even bigger picture, when I feel like this is just on each one of us individually versus, you know, something that we’re all just, like, trying to renegotiate together. When it’s an individual mission, I think that’s maybe when I go escapist mode, when I go, like, fantasize about throwing the phone in the river, I wanna go back in time, back to those New England woods, to no phone. Beck: Back to your fields, back to your compass. Beck: That’s all for this episode of How to Touch Grass. This episode was hosted by me, Julie Beck, and co-hosted and produced by Natalie Brennan. Our editors are Claudine Ebeid and Jocelyn Frank. Fact-check by Gisela Salim-Peyer. Our engineer is Rob Smierciak. Rob also composed some of the music for this show. The executive producer of audio is Claudine Ebeid, and the managing editor of audio is Andrea Valdez. Ashleigh Golden: A priest, a partner, a therapist, or a friend—these people have natural limits. They get tired. They push back. But the chatbot doesn't. Elias Aboujaoude: These programs are trained to be endlessly validating and endlessly agreeable. Beck: Turning to AI for help with anxiety could actually end up making you more anxious. That’s next time on How to Touch Grass.

The Preacher
2026-07-27

The Preacher

The Atlantic | 2026-07-27

Visit Original Website
Read more about the Democrats who might run for president in 2028 here. Governor Wes Moore doesn’t care about saving the Democratic Party; if anything, he thinks it has stood in his way. When we met up in May, he quoted Jay-Z: “Industry shady, it need to be taken over.” The 2001 track is about Jay-Z’s rise to fame, and the ways the recording industry tried to exploit and co-opt him because he was “raisin’ the status quo up.” Moore was talking about a failed political system, and a Democratic Party that, he told me, “tried as hard as they could to get me not to run.” Jay-Z describes himself as a “renegade,” and Moore would like to be seen in a similar light—as the outsider who got himself to the top. Moore is the first Black governor of Maryland and the only Black governor serving in America today. At 47, he’s a relative newcomer to politics—he was the head of Robin Hood, a poverty-fighting foundation, until 2021—but he’s already being discussed as a possible presidential candidate in 2028. He’s just the kind of Bronze Star–winning, God-praising, son-of-an-immigrant Rhodes Scholar whom many Democratic voters would see as a dream candidate (although he denied to me that he was considering running). But so far, his tenure as governor has been far from smooth. In May 2025, he surprised many people by vetoing a bill establishing a commission to study the economic effects of slavery and racism on the state’s citizens and to recommend policies that could include reparations. Black legislators were outraged, and the Maryland General Assembly eventually overrode his veto. Moore defended his decision by dismissing the commission as a body conducting mere academic research, when what people needed was real change: “I am not a believer in studies, commissions,” he told me. “I’m like, Are we doing the work or not?” He pointed to big investments in HBCUs and a mass pardon for cannabis convictions as proof of his progressive accomplishments. Richard D. Kahlenberg: The Democrats can’t let go of racial preferences Moore likes to portray himself as an independent actor, beholden to no one. He has signed legislation that prevents 16- and 17-year-olds from being tried as adults for some crimes, and also increased funding for the police. He has, he boasted to me, vetoed “more bills than any Democratic governor in the history of the state of Maryland” because he doesn’t believe in passing “unfunded mandates.” And he doesn’t care whether the party likes it or not: “I didn’t win because of the Democratic Party,” he said. “I won in spite of the Democratic Party.” He added: “I don’t come from nothing, and I didn’t need nothing, and I’m not owned by anybody. When I’m making my decisions, there is no party boss that I call.” Although this message seems tailor-made for this moment, in which so many Americans are simultaneously highly partisan yet disgusted by both parties, Moore’s approval rating in Maryland has fallen below 50 percent for the first time. (He’s still expected to win easily in the November election—he won 88 percent of the vote in the June primary, and one of his staffers shared with me an internal poll that found a higher approval rating.) He’s facing a number of challenges, particularly the dogged perception that he lacks authenticity—that he hasn’t been honest about who he really is. Moore has a politician’s powder-white smile and a bald head that shines like glass. I met him this spring, in Selma, Alabama, where he’d gone to commemorate the anniversary of Bloody Sunday, when about 600 civil-rights marchers were attacked on the Edmund Pettus Bridge. He began the weekend with a speech at Brown Chapel AME Church. “I know we are built for this, because I know what we are built of,” he told the mostly Black crowd. He listed heroes of the civil-rights movement and then said, “This is our time,” holding up his index finger like a Baptist preacher. Everywhere he went, lines formed. And he played his role perfectly. A bear hug for Governor J. B. Pritzker of Illinois. A selfie for a Black teenager with braces. “Good morning! Good morning! Good morning! How’s everybody doin’?” Then, pulling on the words like a southerner: “Alllll riiiiiight.” An hour or so later, at the Historic Tabernacle Baptist Church, his words tipped toward preaching: “I know where my worthiness comes from, and it does not come from Washington, D.C.” A few weeks later, on the Baltimore Orioles’ Opening Day, he stepped out of a black SUV wearing an orange jersey with his name on the back, and a man rushed up to him: “Sign my baseball for when you run for president.” “Not going to happen,” Moore quickly responded—while signing his name. Over the next few months, I watched Moore go from boat ride to baseball game to matcha tasting to college-graduation speech. In every situation, he knew exactly who to be. By the time he quoted Jay-Z to me, I understood that he had me pegged, too. I was fascinated by Moore’s ability to adapt to his different audiences. I grew up having to do the same, and although I appreciate that I can connect with people from both elite and underprivileged backgrounds, I wish I were as much of a natural as he is. Even as a journalist at The Atlantic, I still find myself sometimes intimidated, my voice trembling. Moore moved like water. But this may be one reason some voters struggle to trust him. When I asked people I know in Maryland, many of them Black, what they think of the governor, I heard things such as He doesn’t say what he means and We don’t know what his agenda is. They thought it was weird that he won without having any political experience. That distrust has been fueled by persistent negative reporting from conservative media outlets. At the Orioles game, a white man in a USA shirt said, “I appreciate you, Gov,” as Moore autographed his collection of baseball cards. Then Moore walked onto the field, and many people in the stadium booed. He smiled and clapped for himself, and seemed to take no notice of the crowd’s displeasure. Moore has been accused of making what The Washington Free Beacon called “a litany of false statements about his family history, his upbringing, and his academic, athletic, and military achievements.” The accusations range from minor to significant, and begin with his hometown. The text on the back cover of his 2010 book, The Other Wes Moore: One Name, Two Fates, reads: “Two kids named Wes Moore were born blocks apart.” Both “grew up fatherless in similar Baltimore neighborhoods and had difficult childhoods.” In fact, Moore’s first years were spent in Takoma Park, Maryland—a nice suburb of Washington, which I know because I live about five minutes away. The book itself does mention that his family home was on “the border of Maryland and Washington, D.C.” But many people who read the book still come away with the impression that Moore was born and raised in Baltimore. It’s true that he grew up fatherless. Moore was 3 when his father died of a virus; he believes that a racist health-care system gave him shoddy treatment. His mother moved with Moore and his sisters to the Bronx, to be closer to her family. He attended the elite Riverdale Country School, but after his grades dropped and he got caught with a smoke bomb in the locker room as a prank, his mother sent him to Valley Forge Military Academy, in Pennsylvania. (His grandparents took out a second mortgage to help pay the tuition.) He moved back to Baltimore only as a college student, to go to Johns Hopkins University. He was living there in 2015 when the city erupted in protests following the death of Freddie Gray, who suffered a spinal-cord injury while inside a police van. Moore co-authored an account of that period with Erica L. Green, Five Days: The Fiery Reckoning of an American City. (“I am from Baltimore,” he wrote in that book.) He later kept a home in the city while commuting to Robin Hood in New York. After his Baltimore bonafides came under scrutiny in 2022, his campaign said that he had asked his publisher to correct the promotional text on The Other Wes Moore, and it did for later editions. Moore quickly learned to be more careful: “I’m not a Baltimorean by birth, but I’m a Baltimorean by choice,” he told a crowd in 2022. But a pattern had been established—or at least a line of attack. In 2010, Stephen Colbert described Moore, a guest on The Colbert Report, as a recipient of a Bronze Star for his service in Afghanistan, where he’d served in 2005 and 2006. The trouble was that he didn’t have a Bronze Star. When reporters raised questions about the medal, Moore’s staff insisted that he’d never told anyone that he had won it, and so he wasn’t responsible for the error. But in 2024, The New York Times obtained Moore’s application for a White House fellowship in 2006 in which he’d written, “the 82nd Airborne Division have awarded me the Bronze Star Medal.” He had an explanation for that too: The senior officer who had helped Moore write the application told him that he was going to get the medal, and so he should include it; the fact that it never actually arrived was embarrassing, but not unusual in the chaos of wartime. And after the controversy surfaced, the military did indeed award him a Bronze Star—just 18 years late. From the July 2026 issue: Being Black in Pete Hegseth’s military Then the Free Beacon ran a story questioning Moore’s account that his great-grandfather had moved to Jamaica because he’d been targeted by the KKK, based on the fact that reporters couldn’t find contemporaneous newspaper or archival accounts to verify it. This charge seemed more than a little forced—as did the many stories about Moore’s thesis at the University of Oxford. In that same White House fellowship application, he wrote that he’d received his degree in 2003 or 2004, but it wasn’t formally recorded until 2005. He also listed the title of his thesis as “The Rise and Ramifications of Radical Islam in the Western Hemisphere,” but it was later titled “Radical Islam in Latin America in the Late 20th Century and Its Middle Eastern Roots.” Also, the thesis is not stored in Oxford’s Bodleian Library—though there is no requirement for theses to be kept there, and many aren’t. Moore told The Baltimore Sun that he wasn’t “going to spend a second of my time trying to dig” a copy up. If you’re a little bored, you’re right to be. Some people have compared Moore to Barack Obama, and the demands for him to release more records to back up his past inevitably reminded me of the “birther” conspiracy. At least that conspiracy had some stakes. How much should anyone care that in 2006, Moore said he’d been named to the Maryland College Football Hall of Fame when in fact he had been honored only by a College Football Hall of Fame affiliate called the National Football Foundation? Moore expresses regret for not being clear about the Bronze Star, but when I asked him about the attacks on his truthfulness in general, he dismissed them. “Do you know who has never attacked my military record? The United States Army,” he said. He told me he lived by a code: “You do not lie, cheat, or steal.” In fact, he said, he was being accused of dishonesty precisely because he was so honest. “It’s basic laws of warfare: You go after your opponent’s strength.” And he pointed out that these accusations surfaced only after he started gaining support. “I went a whole year on a campaign, and no one had a word to say about me,” he said. “And then out of nowhere, it was like the deluge began,” and people from “the right wing started putting stuff out.” The Baltimore Sun, which has devoted much space to these controversies, has indeed moved rightward under a new owner, the television magnate David Smith, and the conservative Free Beacon’s tagline is a promise to cover “the enemies of freedom in the way the mainstream media won’t.” Moore told me that Obama had given him advice on surviving these kinds of attacks as a politician, saying, “Listen, this is the part of the game that everyone hates.” But if the questions about Moore’s honesty have been amplified by his opponents, they still trace back to an uncomfortable set of facts: Moore allowed people to call him a Bronze Star–winning Baltimore kid, when the story was more complicated. In April, one of Moore’s biggest legislative priorities—a redistricting plan that could have helped Democrats pick up a seat in the midterms—died when William Ferguson, the Democratic president of the state Senate, refused to bring it up for a vote. It was “too risky,” Ferguson argued: The new map could be challenged, and a ruling could leave their party worse off. Ferguson is now pushing to get a constitutional amendment before Maryland voters in November that would better shore up any new map from legal challenges. Moore has framed the legislature’s caution as timidity, but Ferguson has a different take. He told me that Moore had created confusion by waiting months before coming out in favor of the plan, and suggested that the governor embraced it only after Gavin Newsom gained national attention for pushing through a new map in California. “I don’t think that timing was a coincidence,” Ferguson said, adding that Moore tends to emphasize “messaging” over governing. “Comms is dictating policy, and policy is not dictating comms.” He did, however, credit Moore with working to speedily rebuild the Francis Scott Key Bridge. Its collapse, in 2024, killed six people and is the most significant catastrophe Moore has weathered as governor. The port of Baltimore was expected to be shut down for many months, but the state got it open again after less than three. Moore is expected to win a second term in November, but the public’s mistrust has taken a political toll. The Sun and others have accused him of not being forthcoming about the state’s budget, which has seesawed from deficit to surplus to deficit again during his tenure. His vetoing of the reparations bill made some of his core supporters feel that he had misled them. When Moore’s approval rating declined, poll respondents listed the budget and the governor’s trustworthiness among their top concerns. Something about Moore does feel protean. He’s hard to place. His background can be confusing. Although he’s often compared to Obama, his education was far less rarefied. Obama was the president of the Harvard Law Review; Moore went to junior college before landing at Johns Hopkins. He describes his upbringing as working-class—“a very hardworking family, but it was definitely a working-class family.” Perhaps by income, but by few other definitions. Both parents were college graduates. His father was an anchorman at a radio station and his mother wrote for radio and TV. Although Moore frequently says that she didn’t have a job with benefits until he was a teenager, she had won a Peabody Award and taught journalism at Howard University before he was born. Still, he saw himself as an outsider, and wouldn’t invite his friends from Riverdale back to his neighborhood. He told me that he thought very strategically about fitting in: “Okay, how do I adapt and adjust so I don’t stick out?” At Valley Forge, he was taught how to mingle with the powerful—how to walk, eat, and speak. As a high-ranking cadet, he attended lunches with former presidents, including Gerald Ford and George H. W. Bush. He said he eventually learned to accept that he was different and to be “comfortable in my own skin.” In two separate conversations, he quoted the same Jay-Z line: “I’m comfortable, dog, Brooklyn to Rome.” But he enunciated every word, in a way that sounded stiff. Despite his charisma onstage, up close, he has no swag. Where he seemed most at home was at the matcha café in Baltimore owned by an old friend of his, who surprised him with a Jamaican sorrel—a tart iced drink. “This right here would make my grandmother proud,” he said. Once, I made a Bernie Mac reference, and he got it immediately, and we shared a knowing laugh. Above all, he was comfortable talking about religion. “I come from a whole long line of preachers and teachers,” he said. “My great-grandfather was a minister; my grandfather was a minister.” In the summers, he used to stay in his grandfather’s parsonage in Brooklyn. When he told his grandmother that he was running for office, she said, “When you’re done with that, maybe you can become a minister.” “Asking me not to talk about or to acknowledge God is like asking me not to breathe air,” he told me. When I asked his mother, Joy Moore, if she thought he would run for president, she said: “I think his faith will take him wherever he’s going to eventually go.” She seemed more annoyed by the questions about his honesty and integrity than Moore was. “They question things that are so personal,” she said. She felt as if the articles interrogating their family history were accusing them all—her grandfather, her father, herself, her son—of being liars. And the Oxford thesis? “I mean, if he knew he was going to one day run for governor, don’t you think he would have kept stuff like that?” Read: Something is happening in the Democratic base Moore says he’d had no plans to go into politics before 2022. It was his wife, Dawn, who was the political operative, a campaign strategist who’d worked for two lieutenant governors. She and Moore eloped when he was on leave from his service in Afghanistan. While they were dating, she was diagnosed with multiple sclerosis, and I was curious about whether she found divulging it to him hard. After all, I know plenty of men who wouldn’t stick around for the common cold. She said she asked him: “How would you feel if you had to take care of me for the rest of your life?” And he said: “How would you feel, if you had to take care of me for the rest of my life?” Some of the questions regarding Moore’s authenticity amount to a nebulous discomfort with how he comes across, a discomfort I recognize from all the times I’ve encountered it myself. Many people who work in elite careers after growing up relatively disadvantaged, as well as Black people who spend time in primarily white spaces, are accused of not being true to themselves. This is a false dichotomy. We can be true to ourselves while also understanding that success requires us to be supple and adaptable, and to present different aspects of our personalities at different times. There’s a famous poem by Paul Laurence Dunbar called “We Wear the Mask,” about the way Black Americans shield themselves from racism. I mentioned it to Moore, and he recognized it immediately. “The mask is heavy,” he said. Moore says he wears his own mask around many people, “not because it makes me more comfortable—it’s because it makes them more comfortable.” Being in spaces where people are “not used to seeing you” is a burden, he said. Being “an enigma” is a burden. “To some people, your presence is inspirational. To certain people, your presence is offensive. To certain people, your presence means progress. To certain people, your presence means we’ve lost something.” You’re a first, or you’re a threat. “That weight does sit on you,” he said. “And it’s heavy.”
The Atlantic
Odysseus’s Confession
2026-07-26

Odysseus’s Confession

The Atlantic | 2026-07-26

Visit Original Website
Toward the end of Homer’s Odyssey, Odysseus and Penelope finally see each other after 20 years apart. Odysseus, disguised as “an ancient old man” by the goddess Athena, tells Penelope that her husband will return to kill the suitors who have tormented her. “Not one will avoid his death and destruction,” he says. This is Homer’s protagonist: wily, aided by a god, and eager for revenge—reflective of a culture that prized honor, strength, and fealty to a set of interventionist deities. This is not, however, Christopher Nolan’s protagonist. At key moments in the recent blockbuster adaptation of the ancient-Greek poem, Odysseus’s story seems more biblical than Homeric. In the film, when Odysseus reunites with Penelope, he hasn’t been turned into an old man. Nolan keeps his identity secret through more naturalistic means: a dimly lit room, a cloak over his body, and latticed sliding doors that separate the two characters. The doors, which are not found in Homer’s poem, are evocative of the screen between priest and penitent in Catholic confession—a setting that Nolan, who was raised Catholic, would be familiar with. And although in Homer’s poem, Odysseus is thirsty for retribution, Nolan emphasizes a different emotion in this scene, one that is integral to the confessional and to Christian life as a whole: contrition. Odysseus describes deep guilt over his central role in a famous episode of the Trojan War: when Greek soldiers hid inside a wooden horse to trick the people of Troy into letting them into their city. “We left them a gift,” he tells his wife, “an offering of peace, that they took into their home.” In sacking Troy, Odysseus says, he and his fellow soldiers “violated all that’s ever sacred between people”—he recognizes a link between mistreating humanity and disrespecting divinity. He confesses that he “lost his way,” not just temporally or physically but in his soul. Other aspects of Nolan’s adaptation feel biblical, even Christian, rather than Homeric—a quasi-baptism scene at sea, for example, and the conspicuous absence of the references to sex that populate the poem. The core principle of the film’s moral universe seems to echo the Bible. “Treat others as you would be treated,” Penelope declares early in the film, expressing an ethic that various characters refer to as “Zeus’s law.” Many versions of the idea exist, in secular and religious forms. But in the film, Penelope’s words sound more like Jesus’s Golden Rule than anything in Homer, where the heroes—though bound to host strangers as if they might be gods in disguise—seek to achieve glory more than convey empathy. Read: Reading a 3,000-year-old poem to a 3-year-old boy The film’s treatment of Odysseus’s lies, too, seems consonant with the Ten Commandments instead of Homer’s moral worldview. In the poem, Odysseus’s lies are often portrayed positively, as evidence of his intelligence and resourcefulness—when he tricks the Cyclops, for example. In the film, however, Odysseus’s falsehoods have dire consequences, an apparent reflection of the biblical assertion that false witness is a sin. Odysseus tasks a young soldier named Sinon—a character who does not appear in the poem—with offering the horse to the Trojans. But he doesn’t tell Sinon that its purpose is to attack the city. Sinon dies as a result of Odysseus’s deception. Later, in Hades, he admonishes his commander: “Did you have to lie to me?” So did Christopher Nolan make a Christian film, which the Daily Wire founder Ben Shapiro and others have suggested since the movie’s release? Not quite. As my colleague Gal Beckerman wrote, Nolan diminishes the role of the gods, treating them as socially useful but not necessarily real. It’s hard to call definitively “Christian” a movie that regards the divine as a possible fantasy. The film is God-haunted, though. Homer may have viewed Odysseus’s deceit and pillaging as glorious. Nolan’s adaptation seems to pass a different judgment. Animated in part by the wisdom of the Bible, this modern Odyssey can’t help but see how the deceptions of war degrade a person and a culture.

News and Politics - Slate Magazine
We Faced Down Trump’s Secret Police in Minneapolis. Here’s How We Did It.
2026-07-27

We Faced Down Trump’s Secret Police in Minneapolis. Here’s How We Did It.

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-07-27

Visit Original Website
This is part of Amicus and Slate’s “By the People” series, which explores how the American people can reclaim the Constitution—and the law—from this monarchical Supreme Court. When Tippy Amundson was in middle school, she read the diary of a teenager hiding from the secret police. “Terrible things are happening outside,” it read. “Families are torn apart; men, women, and children are separated. Children come home from school to find that their parents have disappeared.” Amundson tried to imagine what she would do if she lived during such terrible times. I would totally hide Anne Frank, she reasoned. But when would you ever have the opportunity to do that? This is a civilized world we live in now. This is America. A quarter century later, Amundson, now a 39-year-old travel agent and former kindergarten teacher, received a call from her 13-year-old neighbor. He asked if he could hide in her house if Immigration and Customs Enforcement officers chased him home from his bus stop. It was then she knew that the time had come. Amundson’s teenage neighbor had been considering what he might do if he found himself in ICE’s crosshairs. His parents didn’t want him to go to school; they themselves were afraid to go to work. Every day when he got off the bus, he looked around for unmarked cars filled with masked agents wearing body armor and carrying guns, afraid that they might ask him for his papers or put him in their SUVs and drive away. As he walked up to the door, he checked over his shoulders to see if he was being watched, and he hoped his parents would still be waiting for him inside. Amundson agreed to watch the bus stop when he got home and to hide him if necessary. Their story, which only got more dramatic from there, is a prime example of how the people of Minneapolis responded to unprecedented state-sanctioned lawlessness: They mobilized en masse to protect their neighbors. Early in Donald Trump’s second term, the White House issued mandates of ICE and Border Patrol to arrest 3,000 people a day. Lawmakers were denied entry when they tried to perform their mandated oversight duties at immigrant detention facilities. ICE’s mission broadened to police those opposed to the president. The Department of Homeland Security began to surveil social media posts of Trump’s political opponents, using facial recognition software to create “terrorist” databases of peaceful protesters and following the demonstrators to their houses, sometimes calling them by name. ICE and Border Patrol agents, in masks and unwilling to identify themselves, were empowered by the Supreme Court to demand the papers of people because of their accent, their job, or simply their “apparent race or ethnicity.” In short, according to Lee Morgenbesser, who studies past and present authoritarian regimes and secret police, ICE meets the criteria for a secret police force. Secret police, or something very near that description, swept through Chicago from September to December 2025. Senior Border Patrol commander Greg Bovino, with masked and militarized federal officers, waged a monthslong rampage that saw protesters, first responders, and children tear-gassed, journalists arrested, citizens abducted and shot, and a man killed. It was then that the Trump administration settled its gaze on Minneapolis. A haven for refugees fleeing a civil war in the late ’90s, Minnesota is home to some 80,000 people of Somali descent, most of whom are citizens. “I don’t want them in this country, to be honest with you,” Trump said to his Cabinet and reporters about Americans of Somali descent. “We’re at a tipping point. I don’t know if people mind me saying that, but I’m saying it. We could go one way or the other, and we’re going to go the wrong way if we keep taking in garbage to our country.” On Dec. 2, federal police kidnapped a Metro Transit bus driver on his way to work because he was Somali. The officers unlawfully detained him (or, more accurately, trafficked him) in three different states, and he wouldn’t see his family again for a month. So began Operation Metro Surge, an invasion whose name was inspired by the war on terror and which was conceived to punish a place where Trump is deeply unpopular. “THE DAY OF RECKONING AND RETRIBUTION IS COMING,” the president tweeted in all caps at the height of the occupation. “My kids were literally pointing out masked gunmen on the way to school,” Amundson remembered. Agents with their faces covered up to their eyes, in unmarked black SUVs with tinted windows, would wait outside churches, mosques, schools, daycares, courtrooms, and hospitals. They would sweep neighborhoods where they thought immigrants lived, cornering people alone, rolling up on them and throwing them into their vehicles for spot citizenship checks. Refugees, asylum-seekers with pending applications, green-card holders, citizens, and children alike were swept off the streets and pulled into detention. When I left for work during that period, I would see legal observers in high-vis vests standing outside the school across from my house, keeping watch and signaling to students that they were safe and that they belonged. I drove to my job at a middle school, where more legal observers patrolled outside. In the relative safety inside, attendance plummeted among Black and brown students. Those who did come to school told me about the eyes of masked agents staring at them as they had left their apartments that morning, or the unmarked black SUVs that had followed them on their bus routes the day before to see where the brown kids got off, or the nightmares they had of ICE agents breaking down their doors and taking away their parents and siblings. Some families hid in basements, parents telling their children to avoid going to the first floor at all in case they were spotted through a window. It all came to a head when Renee Nicole Good, a mother and poet two years younger than Amundson, was shot in her car after dropping her 6-year-old off at school. When her wife got out of the car to film and confront ICE officers, Good was told to leave, then ordered to get out of the car. Confused, she started to drive away. That’s when agent Jonathan Ross moved in front of her car and shot her three times: in her arm, chest, and head. According to an independent autopsy, the shots to her arm and chest had not been life-threatening, but the one to her temple had. “Fucking bitch,” Ross said, as the car careered into a pole. Good remained alive for minutes afterward. A bystander begged to help Good, to check her pulse and perform first aid. “I’m a physician!” “Don’t care!” another agent responded. This retort was familiar to Minneapolis residents. It was the message sent by local police officers who refused to let an off-duty firefighter help as officer Derek Chauvin murdered George Floyd. Good was still alive by the time paramedics arrived, but they were unable to save her life. Her family has confirmed that Minneapolis authorities were finally granted access this month to the evidence in her killing, which will allow them to conduct a proper investigation. When Floyd was killed, Minneapolitans were furious. But when Good was killed, our anger was tinged with fear. Many people retreated inside. For some, though, this only strengthened their resolve to monitor agents to make sure they didn’t kill others. Three days after Good’s death, Amundson drove past a Latino grocery store in her neighborhood and saw a group of people sitting in front of the entrance. She pulled over to ask them what was going on. “We think there’s an ICE agent,” someone told her, pointing toward an unmarked car skulking in the parking lot. Amundson went inside the store, where 20 people were hiding. She volunteered to walk them out to their cars, using her white body as a kind of shield. In the parking lot, a legal observer helped her download the encrypted messaging app Signal and join her neighborhood rapid-response group. In the Twin Cities, neighbors tend to know one another, either from one of the 2,000 block parties held during the summer for National Night Out (the event boasts one of the highest attendances in the country), from volunteer work (again, Minnesota has one of the highest rates of any state), or from the logistical coordination required to move snow and ice from public sidewalks, a civic responsibility most Minnesotans take very seriously. This was a fertile environment for the proliferation of digital rapid-response networks at a time when they were most needed. Using Signal, neighbors alerted one another when agents were near. There were large groups that covered swaths of the Twin Cities (all of South Minneapolis, for example), while hyperlocal group chats and voice calls tracked specific neighborhoods. Legal observers, volunteers who sought to surveil the activity of federal agents to ensure safety and compliance with the law, as well as to document who was being taken, came outside when alerted to their presence and blew whistles to warn those nearby. About 30,000 Minnesotans were trained as observers, all intent on making sure that the human beings around them were being treated with basic dignity and given due process. The same week as her trip to the grocery store, Amundson was driving when she saw six masked officers jump out of a car and swarm a man sitting alone at a bus stop. They forced him to empty his pockets and demanded to see his papers. Amundson stopped, got out of her car, and filmed them. “What was I thinking?” she reflected later. How was a “former kindergarten teacher, mom of three, going to take on these masked gunmen?” But she did it anyway. “We’re all trying to do what we can to make these people that we care about feel safe.” Amundson wasn’t an expert on the law, but she knew right from wrong. She was horrified that children as young as 2 were being imprisoned in camps where food is crawling with maggots, where the putrid water makes people sick, where dormant diseases like measles and tuberculosis spread, and where, more frequently than ever, people die. “This is my neighbor,” Amundson explained simply. Atlantic writer Adam Serwer describes what happened in response to Operation Metro Surge as “neighborism,” the ideology that one should care for, and protect, one’s surrounding communities. To me, it captures how people protect one another when the law and its appointed enforcers cannot be trusted. “Who keeps us safe?” began a popular call-and-response protest chant I first heard during the George Floyd uprising and that resurfaced during Metro Surge. “We keep us safe.” On Jan. 22, Amundson and her neighbor Heather Zemian were driving together, patrolling their neighborhood and ensuring that kids were getting home safely after school, when they saw masked agents with guns clustered near the area where kids would soon be getting dropped off by buses. They honked the horn nine times to alert others nearby. The agents approached their car and warned them to stop. Zemian and Amundson complied and had started to exit the parking lot when a local police officer drove in. They pulled over and briefly asked the officer if she had been called in because of them. The officer told them no. “Do I recommend not getting involved? Yes,” she added, abdicating all responsibility for the safety of the neighborhood. “We’re getting involved. They’re our neighbors,” Amundson responded nonconfrontationally. “If ICE shows up to my house because they think neighbors are inside my house, should I call you?” Amundson went on to ask, the question ever present in her mind. Who keeps us safe? But before the officer could elucidate a satisfying response, agents drove up and surrounded Zemian and Amundson’s vehicle. They asked the women if they had called the police, then ripped them out of the car into the subzero air and put handcuffs around their wrists. As they did so, another masked agent in tactical gear filmed them with a professional camera. The police officer watched Zemian and Amundson be taken away in an unmarked car. As they were being driven off, the women noticed that something was wrong with the agent in the front passenger seat. “I’m not feeling so well,” he said, before sliding down and convulsing. The other young agents were frozen, unsure what to do. “He’s having a seizure,” Amundson said. At first they ignored her. “Call fucking 911!” Zemian instructed them. Finally, they pulled over. “He needs help. I have some medical training,” Amundson told them. “You need to uncuff me so I can put him in the recovery position.” They did. Amundson, now uncuffed, walked to the side of the seizing agent, a man who had terrorized her own children. She eased the seat back and took the gun from his hip so he could lie on his side. His mask had slid off, and Amundson was taken aback by how young he was. She began soothing him. Zemian, who had also been uncuffed, started digging through the SUV, collecting spare tactical vests, then propped them underneath the agent’s head. “Hi, my name’s Tippy,” Amundson said. “What’s your name?” He told her. “This was between seizures,” she told me later. “I watched him have three seizures. That ICE agent could have choked on his tongue had I not been there.” In front of her was a human in need. The people nearby didn’t know what to do, so she did what she thought anybody would: She helped him. Eventually, the young agent was taken away by paramedics. Amundson and Zemian’s hopes that their behavior would warrant some leniency were quickly dashed. They were handcuffed again and taken to the Whipple Federal Building. “Because you were so nice to us, we’ll give you your phone call,” the secret police told the women as they drove them to the site where ICE agents were detaining citizens and noncitizens alike. They were charged with obstruction. (Eventually, those charges were dropped.) As they left, their citations in hand, an agent in charge said, “Tell everyone how nice we were to you.” Two days after Amundson’s arrest, ICE agents on the streets of Minneapolis shot Alex Pretti 10 times in the back. Pretti’s last act had been to try to defend someone from pepper spray. Amundson watched the video, and what struck her most was that another agent started clapping and laughing after the 10 shots were fired. Even after the second killing in a month, Amundson continued to observe her neighbor getting off the bus. As civil resistance grew, ICE agents were eventually forced to change their approach from military occupation to a new phase that relied on covert tactics and the collaboration of local law enforcement. People kept disappearing, but schools again filled with traumatized students. “The kids are playing again, and it feels lighter,” Amundson told me months later. “How wonderful it is that nobody need wait a single moment before starting to improve the world,” a 15-year-old in Nazi Germany marveled, before she was taken by the secret police to a concentration camp and killed. “It’s really a wonder that I haven’t dropped all my ideals, because they seem so absurd and impossible to carry out. Yet I keep them, because in spite of everything, I still believe that people are really good at heart.” In the week after my own detainment by the secret police, I took time off work because I was afraid to leave the house. Out the window, I noticed a spot in the alley where the thawing and freezing of ice had created a ravine, where car tires would get lodged and trap the driver. Three separate times, cars got stuck. Hearing the futile spinning of tires, neighbors came out en masse to figure out how to solve the problem. People sprinkled cat litter, got mats out of their own vehicles to place under the tires. Once, someone laid down a broken door, which they had stored in their basement, underneath a car. Despite my fear, I got out of the house. I knocked on the doors of more neighbors to come out and push together. We did what we could with the resources we had, because being a neighbor means watching out for one another. What do you do when the police are doing roving citizen checks on your brown neighbors? When constitutional rights are suspended and the orders of judges ignored by secret police? When doors are battered in without judicial warrants? When citizens are dragged out into the street in their boxers? When children are put in camps? When people are shot in the street for the crime of compassion? You find your neighbors, go outside, and push. Slate relies on advertising to support our journalism. Minneapolis Just Showed the Entire Country How to Take the Constitution Back
2026-07-27

Minneapolis Just Showed the Entire Country How to Take the Constitution Back

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-07-27

Visit Original Website
This is part of Amicus and Slate’s “By the People” series, which explores how the American people can reclaim the Constitution—and the law—from this monarchical Supreme Court. This January, a neighbor texted me a video, along with a warning to avoid our neighborhood. The video showed dozens of people following a black SUV past my home in Minneapolis, blowing whistles and shouting. It was not quite a protest chant, but a verbal alarm for the neighborhood. People came to windows, porches, and icy sidewalks in below-freezing weather because someone had seen Immigration and Customs Enforcement agents in the vehicle, and the new ground rule was simple: None of our neighbors should be taken by ICE without witnesses. This scene captured a common neighborhood response during Operation Metro Surge. There were whistles, Signal chats, legal observers, carpools, rent funds, and grocery deliveries. Warnings passed from block to block, orally and digitally. Some parents kept children home; other children insisted that their parents stay home instead. Residents relayed when not to drive, when not to return, when to stay inside, and when to come out in force. My experience was not exceptional, and that was the point. The surge made public safety a daily practice for many residents. None of it sounded like law in a traditional register. Rather, it was accompaniment: feeding, sheltering, driving, watching, and warning so no one had to move in isolation. What we saw in Minneapolis this past winter was, among other extraordinary things, popular constitutionalism in action. Popular constitutionalism usually names the idea that constitutional meaning is not the province of courts alone. Movements argue about the Constitution, organize around it, and sometimes make claims powerful enough that courts later recognize them as law. Operation Metro Surge belongs to that tradition, but at a more immediate and less familiar level. Residents were not waiting for judicial recognition. They were not claiming the state’s police power for themselves but acting as though they already had a legitimate claim to ensuring the entire community’s safety. The state of Minnesota translated these safety claims into a federal lawsuit. Along with Minneapolis and St. Paul, it challenged Operation Metro Surge by arguing that the federal government had disrupted schools, businesses, emergency services, municipal resources, streets, and the trust on which local governance depends. The complaint invoked Minnesota’s “inviolable sovereign authority” and its duty to ensure the “health, education, and safety” of residents. Translated into constitutional terms, Minnesota argued that the surge violated federalist principles by invading the state’s reserved police powers: the broad authority to govern public health, safety, welfare, and order. That claim translated the fear and disruption of the surge into a constitutional injury that courts could recognize. Yet in Minneapolis, that translation carried its own history. This is the city where police murdered George Floyd, and where the resulting uprising remade the national vocabulary of police violence. Here, the promise that local law enforcement keeps people safe is often hard for residents to take at face value. For many in the city, the problem posed by Operation Metro Surge was not simply whether federal agents had displaced local police. It was that public safety had to be routed through either federal or state policing for the claim to become legible. The whistles and the mutual aid networks demonstrated that it did not. Recent reporting on the Minneapolis Police Department’s response to the surge has sharpened this point. Residents called for help or to report masked agents, guns pointed at protestors, the unrestrained use of chemical munitions, and what looked like kidnappings in the street. The city spent tens of millions in police overtime and standby during the surge, while police rarely responded directly to immigration-related emergency calls. Much of the overtime money the department spent went instead toward preparing for unrest, monitoring protests and vigils, guarding hotels housing federal agents, and managing the public response to federal violence. Local police power did not simply fail to protect residents from federal enforcement—it sought to manage and limit residents’ response to that violence. Resident safety practices are not alien to constitutional history. Long before modern professional police forces, public order depended on watches, constables, alarms, and forms of compelled or expected community response. The old common-law “hue and cry” is not a model to romanticize; it could be coercive, punitive, and exclusionary. But it does show that the state’s current monopoly over the legal meaning of public safety is not natural. Residents’ safety practices during Operation Metro Surge did not revive a bygone era, but exposed the contingency of the present one: the modern assumption that public safety becomes legally cognizable only when the state performs it. The practices in this period changed as enforcement changed. A warning system would form around one pattern of ICE activity, and then the pattern would change. A route became dangerous, then known, then avoided. A license plate circulated on chat networks, and agents later swapped it out. Each day, people adjusted to federal movement, ICE and Customs and Border Protection adjusted to being watched, and people adjusted again. This was not insurgency. It was the refusal of pacification. As policing theorist Mark Neocleous has argued, pacification is the production of order by the very forces that create the danger they claim to manage. It makes official violence look calm and inevitable while popular response is made to look like disorder. Operation Metro Surge was announced as a law enforcement campaign, but many residents experienced it as an effort to pacify the city through fear and overwhelming force. Residents refused to play along. The struggle over public safety became concrete when residents began placing makeshift barricades in the streets to slow ICE vehicles. They were formed with cones, shipping pallets, chairs, trash bins, or even neighbors inside a traffic circle. These were small and temporary impediments, but they worked well enough to make a vehicle pause, and to give residents time to check its license plate against a database, see who was inside, and record and relay the information. The city described the barricades through the language of safety as well. When makeshift roadblocks appeared, Minneapolis urged residents to stop erecting them, citing the need for emergency access, fire response, and roadways open for all. City workers cleared them as quickly as they appeared. Of course, these concerns were real. A blocked street can delay an ambulance or endanger someone whose emergency has nothing to do with immigration enforcement. But the city’s response to the barricades did not settle the public safety question. It revealed an assumption in law about who may act in the name of safety. Police may close roads, establish perimeters, kettle crowds, redirect traffic, block movement, and call those acts public order. Residents doing the same acquire different labels: obstruction, disorder, interference, and even conspiracy. Put differently, law does not begin by asking what the barricade does. It first asks who built it. The state may control movement because it bears the police power, and it bears the police power because it is the state. Once that axiom is treated as constitutional common sense, public safety appears already on the side of the enforcement vehicle. This is where Minnesota’s lawsuit and Minneapolis residents’ barricade response pulled against each other. In court, the state and cities argued that Operation Metro Surge had invaded state and municipal authority. In the street, the city sought to reclaim that authority from residents. Public safety could be local rather than federal, but still institutional rather than popular. The popular response to Operation Metro Surge did not resolve that tension by choosing the city over the federal government. It rejected the assumption that both shared: that public safety must remain an institutional monopoly. Of course, popular claims to community safety can become dangerous. They have justified lynching; border vigilantism; racial, gendered, and xenophobic exclusion; neighborhood surveillance; and mob violence. That history is why contesting the constitutional allocation of public safety cannot mean giving private people authority to govern vulnerable people. The question is not whether state power or popular power is always safer. Neither is. The harder question is what each is doing. What power is being exercised, against whom, by what means, and in response to what danger? In Minneapolis, residents perceived the danger not as the vulnerable populations being stopped, but as armed federal power. The means they deployed were not violent resistance, but warning, watching, accompanying, documenting, delaying, material support, and refusing isolation. That is the popular constitutional contest the whistles, warnings, carpools, legal observers, and barricades made visible. Federal agents claimed public safety by conducting dragnet stops. Local officials claimed public safety by managing the response. Residents claimed public safety by slowing that movement and making enforcement visible. Their practices showed that public safety cannot be measured only by who has legal authority to act in its name. Safety “by the people, for the people” then, is not private policing or blind faith in the crowd. It is the insistence that public safety be judged by the people who must live with its consequences.
